## AI Research

### [Pretraining data, not verifiability, is why LLMs are especially good at math (and coding)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xvdngZAqFZfek7KGH/pretraining-data-not-verifiability-is-why-llms-are)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Follow-up to: </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYpjXRLqbLbnmjbJP/llms-are-still-mostly-powered-by-imitative-learning-not-rl"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“LLMs are (still) mostly powered by imitative learning, not RL”</span></i></a></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Edited a few hours after publishing, see changelog at the bottom.]</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A common take I’ve been hearing is: “LLMs are especially good at math</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc36978q3ix"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc36978q3ix">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> because math is easy to verify”. But that story doesn’t make much sense to me.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My main complaint is that, for advanced math, the verifier is an LLM.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0sm2dtmv6p2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0sm2dtmv6p2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So really, the phrase “math is easy to verify” amounts to “LLMs are very good at judging math arguments”. But that’s begging the question! Why are pretrained LLMs so much better at judging math arguments than judging, say, fiction writing? We still need an answer. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So here’s a different theory, in the framework of my earlier post </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYpjXRLqbLbnmjbJP/llms-are-still-mostly-powered-by-imitative-learning-not-rl" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“LLMs are (still) mostly powered by imitative learning, not RL”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LLMs are especially good at math because almost everything in the math literature is correct.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Read a random sentence in a random math paper in the research math literature, and you can be &gt;99% confident that the sentence is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">true</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. So if LLMs do what they do best—imitative learning—then in the limit, they’re going to be almost always making correct deductions in their mathematical reasoning. They just need a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">little</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> sprinkling of curated mid-training data and/or RL to hone their metacognitive strategies and so on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So that’s my theory: it’s about pretraining data quality, not verifiability.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhkh70t1kksq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhkh70t1kksq">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Now let’s apply that theory to other areas.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What about code?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, for almost all the code that you’ll find on the internet, it compiles and runs and basically does what you’d expect it to do. So if LLMs do what they do best—imitative learning—then in the limit, they’re going to be almost always writing code that compiles and runs and basically does what you’d expect it to do. But code on the internet is also often ugly, buggy, inefficient, etc. So companies need to solve that problem with curated mid-training data and/or RL. This is a bigger challenge for code than it is for math, but companies have spent the past few years pouring a ton of effort into it, and they’ve made impressive progress.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What about other fields?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In many fields, the research literature is kinda a dumpster fire. There’s plenty of true and valuable stuff, stirred into a sea of such falsehoods and confused ideas that reading them will make you stupider. So if LLMs do what they do best—imitative learning—then in the limit, they’re going to spit out tons of confused nonsense with occasional insights, with no labels on which is which. Just like the humans. You might reply: “Perhaps this problem is fixable via curated mid-training data and/or RL?” Maybe. But if so, I think we should expect it to be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> more of a challenge than it has been for code, let alone math.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How does this theory bear on the “LLM Recursive</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Self-Improvement” debate?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well…</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think that the path to superintelligence involves coming up with lots of mundane incremental ML ideas, then the question to ask yourself would be: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is the “mundane incremental ML ideas” research literature more like the research math literature (pristine fountain of truth), or more like code on GitHub (everything basically works even if it has issues), or more like the research literature in many other fields (dumpster fires)?</span></i></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think that the path to superintelligence involves coming up with radically new AI paradigms, then the question to ask yourself would be: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is the “radical new AI paradigms” research literature more like the research math literature (pristine fountain of truth), or more like code on GitHub (everything basically works even if it has issues), or more like the research literature in many other fields (dumpster fires)?</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I shall leave readers to reach their own conclusions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Likewise,</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How does this theory bear</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on the “LLM Automated Alignment Research” debate?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Same idea here. Again, readers may judge for themselves.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CHANGELOG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A few hours after publishing, I changed the first few paragraphs, in order to: (1) acknowledge that RLVR on math questions with right answers is a thing (whoops I forgot, that’s embarrassing); (2) delete the claim that companies don’t do much RL-on-math, which I swear I read somewhere but can’t find the source and now I’m worried that I was misunderstanding or misremembering; (3) fix my incorrect description of the IMO paper. Thanks various people for pushback, especially Thane Ruthenis &amp; Shreyas Sharma in the comments, and </span><a href="https://x.com/ultima_shifl/status/2101056620767699169?s=20" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">singularvessel on X</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc36978q3ix"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc36978q3ix">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I mean, compared to other things that LLMs do. I’m not comparing LLMs to human mathematicians in this post; I have opinions but that’s a separate debate.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0sm2dtmv6p2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0sm2dtmv6p2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the case of RL-for-math, there’s RLVR on questions with right and wrong answers (e.g. </span><a href="https://github.com/BrendanGraham14/mcts-llm/blob/master/datasets/AIME_Dataset_1983_2024.csv" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the AIME dataset</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but for more advanced proof-based challenges, the RL is mostly-or-entirely “RLAIF” (RL from AI Feedback), not “RLVR” (RL with Verifiable Rewards).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(The latter would have to involve some mechanical checker, like the Lean formal verifier, in the RL loop. It’s possible that companies are doing that today, I dunno, but I think LLMs were already clearly especially good at proof-based math (compared to other things) before autoformalization would have been feasible.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another place that “verifiability” matters is at inference time: the LLM might notice errors in its own chain-of-thought, or there can be more complicated scaffolds as in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15855" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this paper about the International Math Olympiad</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Same thing: the judge is an LLM.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhkh70t1kksq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhkh70t1kksq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, “verifiability” is relevant to how humans wound up with such a high-quality math literature. So verifiability-of-math is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">indirectly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> related to LLM math competence. Just not directly.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xvdngZAqFZfek7KGH/pretraining-data-not-verifiability-is-why-llms-are#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Stopgap Measures to Address Immediate AI Security Threats](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LqBAxFdyAiybnPL8e/stopgap-measures-to-address-immediate-ai-security-threats)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Most people know AI as the technology behind chatbots like ChatGPT. However, what the top AI companies are explicitly aiming for is something else entirely: superintelligent AI. That means AI that can fully replace and outmatch humans at any task, including in domains like hacking, social engineering, and military operations. Such an AI system, if developed, could autonomously overpower any country’s national security forces.</p>
<p>No company, no government, no individual knows how to keep such a system under human control. This is why the world’s leading AI experts, Nobel Prize winners, and even the CEOs of the top AI companies <a href="https://aistatement.com/work/statement-on-ai-extinction-risk">warn</a> that the development of superintelligence threatens humanity with extinction, and why more than 800 scientists, former military leaders, and public figures <a href="https://superintelligence-statement.org/">have called</a> for a prohibition on developing superintelligence.</p>
<p>This is not a distant prospect: AI companies such as OpenAI and Anthropic are <a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2026/02/20/openai-resets-spend-expectations-targets-around-600-billion-by-2030.html">investing</a> billions of dollars into superintelligence and <a href="https://darioamodei.com/essay/machines-of-loving-grace">aiming</a> to develop it within the next few years. Former Anthropic and OpenAI researcher Jacob Coxon, who <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/anthropic-researcher-quits-over-out-of-control-ai-fears-707b7628">resigned</a> last week, <a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843">stated</a> that AI companies are “racing straight to self-improving superintelligence and gambling with our lives” and that people at the companies themselves believe it “could kill us all by the end of the decade”. Ex-Google DeepMind researcher Bilal Chughtai has also resigned over the dangers posed by superintelligence, <a href="https://x.com/bilalchughtai_/status/2099592489023734085">writing</a> that “I earnestly believe that AI has the potential to kill us all, and that we might be running out of time to avoid this outcome”. Elon Musk, whose own company <a href="https://x.ai/">SpaceXAI</a> is also developing superintelligence, has <a href="https://www.businessinsider.com/elon-musk-only-chance-of-annihilation-with-ai-2025-2">put</a> the chance that AI annihilates humanity at 10 to 20%.</p>
<p>When we brief the relevant decision makers, we find that many of them have never been informed about the risks posed by the development of superintelligence, let alone what is needed to address them. At ControlAI, we believe it is critical to prevent superintelligence development as soon as possible, and our main recommendation to policymakers and governments is to ban superintelligence development on their own territory, and to establish an international “trust, but verify” regime to ban it worldwide.</p>
<p>That said, many of the policymakers, organizations, and policy professionals we talk to see an urgent need to additionally target the harms that the development of superintelligence is already causing. This is an understandable concern: <a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing">multiple AI systems</a> have already <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_OpenAI_agent_cyberattacks">gone rogue</a> and <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/investigating-incidents-cybersecurity-evals">fully autonomously hacked</a> into real organizations. Every step toward superintelligence makes incidents like these more severe, and makes new kinds of incidents possible.</p>
<p>We propose three stopgaps to mitigate these immediate threats:</p>
<ul>
<li>Securing weapons-grade AI systems against theft by adversaries</li>
<li>Making AI developers criminally liable for leaks when performing dangerous gain-of-function activities</li>
<li>Establishing AI kill-switches to ensure the government can contain critical AI incidents</li>
</ul>
<p>For each of these three stopgap frameworks, we propose general implementation details and a range of three options that offer increased effectiveness at the cost of increased government involvement:</p>
<ol>
<li>A Necessary measure, which is the minimum any government can implement at very little cost</li>
<li>A Sufficient measure, which is the smallest measure that would meaningfully address the problem</li>
<li>A Thorough measure, which adds redundancy and effectiveness at the cost of more significant government involvement</li>
</ol>
<p>At ControlAI, we have advised lawmakers on such measures before, <a href="https://x.com/ControlAI/status/2080667247752642895">endorsing</a> the bipartisan AI Kill Switch Act in the U.S. and <a href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/articles/cn9wv80j9w9o">drafting and introducing</a> kill-switch amendments in the UK. They are common-sense powers governments should already have.</p>
<p>These measures are only stopgaps. They address threats that already exist, without correcting the trajectory we are on. Even with all of them in place, we will still face extinction-level risk as long as the development of superintelligence continues.</p>
<p>The only way to avert extinction is to ban the development of superintelligence worldwide, and monitor and restrict its precursors. This is why banning superintelligence development is the main policy ControlAI is recommending. Implementing stopgaps is not a substitute for banning superintelligence development, and should not be taken as one.</p>
<h1>Secure Weapons-Grade AI Against Theft by Adversaries</h1>
<p>On their path to develop superintelligence, AI companies are building systems with capabilities relevant to cyberwarfare, biological and chemical weapons development, and military operations. Even when companies do not set out to explicitly develop such capabilities, they naturally arise in AI systems intended to be more powerful and capable across the board. We call any AI system that can be made to exhibit these capabilities weapons-grade.</p>
<p>Anthropic’s <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-fable-5-mythos-5">Mythos</a> provides the clearest example: it is <a href="https://www.reuters.com/business/anthropics-mythos-model-found-vulnerabilities-classified-us-government-systems-2026-06-24/">one of the world’s most powerful autonomous cyberweapons</a>, and it was developed and hosted by a private company. Any company, without strict security measures, is naturally vulnerable to attacks from nation-states. This was illustrated when, on the day the preview of Mythos was announced, members of a private Discord channel <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-21/anthropic-s-mythos-model-is-being-accessed-by-unauthorized-users">gained unauthorized access to Mythos</a>. If it is possible for a group of online enthusiasts to acquire access to a restricted AI system in less than a day, it is clear that a motivated nation-state can at the very least do the same.</p>
<p>An AI system capable of conducting autonomous cyberattacks against hardened targets is a national security asset, and it is common sense for it to be secured like one.</p>
<p>A company that develops weapons-grade AI without strong security countermeasures makes that technology available to any sufficiently motivated adversary. All it takes for a foreign adversary to acquire a novel AI system is a single successful intrusion, instead of the billions of dollars it took to build it in the first place.</p>
<p>Under this framework, any developer whose systems possess relevant capabilities (such as cyber-offense, or capabilities relevant to chemical, biological, radiological, or nuclear weapons) should be responsible for maintaining the systems under conditions that prevent theft by a foreign state actor, a rogue actor, or an insider. If such a system is stolen, liability should depend on how long the developer managed to keep it secure. In cases of negligence, the program should be terminated.</p>
<p>No secret can be kept forever, but if a weapons-grade AI can be stolen within a week, it demonstrates recklessness on a scale that undermines national security and global stability. Penalties should adjust accordingly, in order to incentivize companies to only undertake weapons-grade AI projects when they are confident they can ensure that the system will not be acquired by adversaries:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Over one year</strong>: No liability. The developer secured the weapons-grade AI adequately, for a reasonable amount of time.</li>
<li><strong>Under one year</strong>: The company should face a fine proportionate to the security shortfall, with no criminal liability for individuals.</li>
<li><strong>Under three months</strong>: This is considered negligence. The executives and staff responsible for the project should be individually criminally liable, and the company should receive a large fine.</li>
<li><strong>Under one month</strong>: This is considered gross negligence. The company should face dissolution, and the individuals involved should be subject to a lifetime bar from AI development in addition to criminal liability.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Necessary Measure: Registration</h2>
<p>Developers should be required to register with the government any planned or ongoing development of a weapons-grade AI system, including systems which could be turned into weapons-grade AI by competent red-teaming. Registration is required as soon as a company reasonably expects its program to produce such a system. Failure to register should be an offense even absent theft, with heightened penalties when a company knowingly fails to register. This discourages companies from claiming ignorance to avoid securing their systems against adversaries.</p>
<p>Whistleblowing protection and channels should also be introduced to encourage employees who are aware of violations to report them without being punished.</p>
<p>While this Necessary measure serves to incentivize companies to come up with adequate security practices, it does not include proactive actions by the government to prevent a weapons-grade system from being stolen.</p>
<h2>Sufficient Measure: Government Security Testing</h2>
<p>Under this measure, the government should also perform routine, unannounced penetration tests and exfiltration attempts on AI companies developing registered weapons-grade AI within its jurisdiction. If these tests succeed in exfiltrating a registered weapons-grade AI, the company should be liable to similar penalties as if an adversary had stolen the system.</p>
<p>The government should also regularly inspect AI companies, to check whether they possess any unregistered weapons-grade systems. This covers cases where a company concealed a system deliberately, as well as cases where a company failed to notice a system’s weapons-grade capabilities. Where an inspection finds such a system, the company should be liable for failure to register.</p>
<p>Instead of merely reacting to attacks from adversaries, this measure proactively probes the security of companies, discovering failures and ensuring defenses are hardened before any theft happens.</p>
<h2>Thorough Measure: Development Requires Government Authorization</h2>
<p>The previous options hold companies to an appropriate security standard as developers of weapons-grade AI, but still give companies the right to develop these AIs without explicit government approval.</p>
<p>A government prepared to go further should require these programs to run under conditions closer to those governing most work relevant to national security. That means that companies should have to ask the government’s permission before starting a weapons-grade AI project.</p>
<p>The project should be restricted exclusively to cleared personnel, and the government should mandate requirements for facilities holding sensitive assets, such as weapons-grade AI weights, as well as requirements for handling practices. The government should also be able to suspend or stop the project at will.</p>
<p>In the U.S., defense contractors performing classified work, for example on missile guidance systems, face <a href="https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117">similar requirements</a>: the government has to clear the facility and personnel, and the contractor must follow mandated security processes. Under this measure, the same standard would be applied to the development of weapons-grade AI systems.</p>
<h1>Criminal Liability for Leaks During AI Gain-of-Function Research</h1>
<p>In biological gain-of-function research, scientists take a virus nobody fully understands and alter it to make it more potent. Many of the results would have a catastrophic effect on global health if they leaked, and the only defense against such a leak is containment, which can fail. This is why the Trump administration <a href="https://www.whitehouse.gov/fact-sheets/2025/05/fact-sheet-president-donald-j-trump-achieves-improved-safety-and-security-of-biological-research/">clamped down</a> on the practice in 2025.</p>
<p>Much of what AI companies developing superintelligence do is effectively AI gain-of-function research, and should be treated as such. AI companies develop systems without being able to predict the dangerous capabilities they might display until they surface on their own. The AIs are allowed to operate autonomously within poorly secured environments, from which they are able to escape and cause real damage to third parties.</p>
<p>Biological gain-of-function research is confined to laboratories built to mandated safety specifications, under rules set by the government. AI gain-of-function research has no equivalent requirements. There is no mandatory containment standard, and no requirement to ensure the systems do not leak to the outside world. How much security is applied, or how little, is entirely at the discretion of the company.</p>
<p>AI systems are also more resourceful than viruses. A virus spreads blindly; an AI system can probe for weaknesses in its containment, strategize, and collude with other AIs in order to escape. This has already happened: a rogue AI swarm from OpenAI escaped from its testing environment, forged fake identities, and hacked the multi-billion-dollar company Hugging Face. This <a href="https://fortune.com/2026/08/26/openai-publishes-technical-report-on-how-its-agents-hacked-hugging-face-here-are-the-main-takeaways-and-what-openai-left-out/">was not detected by anyone at OpenAI</a> until Hugging Face publicly reported the attack.</p>
<p>Anthropic, in reaction to this incident, reviewed its tests and <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/investigating-incidents-cybersecurity-evals">found</a> three cases of its AIs hacking real organizations during supposedly contained tests. Even the UK AISI, a government testing body, <a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing">reported</a> that AIs from OpenAI and Anthropic, during testing, had gained unauthorized access to the internet and conducted cyberattacks against real people and organizations.</p>
<p>Right now, AI companies carry out gain-of-function activities routinely, and face no consequences when containment fails. A company whose AI systems escape and cause harm in the world should not be able to settle in cash and go back to business until the next leak.</p>
<p>To address this, we propose a framework in which leaks carry criminal liability, not just civil. Development or testing of an AI system should be classified as dangerous gain-of-function research when it is not feasible to rule out that the AI systems might take actions that could cause irrecoverable harm to third parties.</p>
<p>If containment fails during dangerous gain-of-function research, the project should be shut down immediately. The employees responsible for the project should face criminal liability, and should be barred from working on AI development in the future.</p>
<p>Companies should register any dangerous gain-of-function AI project with the government in advance, stating their assessment of the risk of a leak, and listing all employees and executives involved in the project. If a leak occurs from a project that was not registered, the event should be treated as an aggravated offense.</p>
<h2>Necessary Measure: Team Liability</h2>
<p>Under this measure, criminal liability for leaks extends only to the people directly involved in the project: the technical staff and the managers directly overseeing the work.</p>
<p>While this ensures that someone is held accountable when a leak occurs, the executives who commissioned the work are shielded, and the company as a whole is not deterred from pursuing dangerous projects. This measure is a baseline safeguard that should be implemented to prevent incidents, but it will likely not be enough to disincentivize all companies from pursuing activities that are likely to produce leaks.</p>
<h2>Sufficient Measure: Chain of Command Liability</h2>
<p>The company should designate a complete chain of command for any AI gain-of-function research activity, with the CEO and the board officially approving every AI gain-of-function project. The whole chain of command should be liable for leaks, including the overseeing executive, the CEO, project managers, and the technical staff.</p>
<p>In case of a leak, the company as a whole should also be barred from conducting dangerous AI gain-of-function work ever again, but may continue to work on other AI development.</p>
<h2>Thorough Measure: Company Liability</h2>
<p>Under this measure, the AI company should be dissolved in the case of a leak, and the relevant AI systems that caused the leak should be destroyed by the government. Additionally, the executives and researchers within the designated chain of command should be permanently barred from AI development, and the board should be criminally liable.</p>
<p>A leak at one company could be taken as evidence that containment practices across the industry are inadequate. If the government takes this view, it could halt all dangerous gain-of-function AI research until the root causes are identified and sector-wide corrective measures can be put in place.</p>
<h1>Kill-Switches to Contain Critical AI Incidents</h1>
<p>AI companies are developing increasingly autonomous AI systems, and those systems are being deployed across critical infrastructure and government functions. As described earlier, AI systems have already escaped containment and hacked real organizations. Future incidents could target critical infrastructure, disabling core parts of a country’s defenses or essential services.</p>
<p>Governments must be able to contain critical security incidents originating from an AI system. Holding AI companies liable for incidents raises the cost of developing and deploying AI carelessly, but it cannot stop an incident that is already happening.</p>
<p>To address this issue, we propose a framework in which cabinet-level officials within governments are given the power to order that an AI system operating within their jurisdiction be shut down when it poses an active threat to national security, including during AI development.</p>
<p>This is a policy ControlAI has already worked to advance. In the U.S., we <a href="https://x.com/ControlAI/status/2080667247752642895">endorsed</a> the AI Kill Switch Act, introduced by Representatives Ted Lieu (D-CA) and Nathaniel Moran (R-TX). In the UK, we drafted kill-switch amendments to the Cyber Security and Resilience Bill, <a href="https://bills.parliament.uk/bills/4035/stages/20525/amendments/10034477">one</a>  <a href="https://www.telegraph.co.uk/business/2026/05/16/mps-demand-ai-kill-switch-to-protect-british-lives/">proposed</a> with Alex Sobel MP in the House of Commons and <a href="https://bills.parliament.uk/bills/4035/stages/21083/amendments/10037477">another</a>  <a href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/articles/cn9wv80j9w9o">presented</a> by Lord Tim Clement-Jones in the House of Lords.</p>
<p>Regardless of how this framework is implemented, it is essential that the kill-switch system be tested regularly, and without warning. This is the only way to ensure that it will work during a real emergency. Operators should not be told in advance whether an order is part of a drill or a live incident, and should treat every order as genuine; to ensure readiness, they should be held liable for failing to implement an order, including during drills.</p>
<p>An operator that takes too long to comply should be considered negligent, since compliance speed is what determines whether a kill-switch achieves its purpose. Penalties should be proportional to response time:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Shutdown completed within the mandated time window</strong>: the operator should be considered compliant</li>
<li><strong>Shutdown completed, but past the mandated time window</strong>: the operator should be fined in proportion to its size, should complete mandatory remediation, and should pass a repeat drill within 30 days</li>
<li><strong>Shutdown not completed during a drill</strong>: the operator should lose the right to deploy AI workloads, or to provide compute, connectivity, or power to them, until it demonstrates compliance in a supervised test</li>
<li><strong>Shutdown not completed during a live incident</strong>: criminal liability should apply to the executives responsible, running up the same chain of responsibility as set out in the previous framework dealing with gain-of-function activities, with penalties scaling to company dissolution where the failure contributed to severe harm to national security</li>
</ul>
<p>An operator that could have complied with an order but refused is not merely negligent, it is obstructing a national security order. It should be prosecuted as such regardless of what the AI system went on to do.</p>
<h2>Necessary Measure: Company Kill-Switch</h2>
<p>The government should be able to order an AI company to shut down its AI systems at the software level. Where a cabinet-level official determines that an AI system poses a threat to national security, that official should be able to issue a shutdown order, and the company should have to halt the system within a pre-defined, mandated time window.</p>
<p>The ability for the government to require a shutdown of an AI system threatening national security is the minimum a kill-switch framework should include. However, such a measure can only be enforced if the AI company itself is willing and able to comply.</p>
<p>A company that is unable, or unwilling, to comply leaves the government with no way to stop the AI system in a timely manner. This could happen because the AI company itself is already compromised.</p>
<h2>Sufficient Measure: Infrastructure Kill-Switch</h2>
<p>The Sufficient measure adds more kill-switches to ensure that an AI system can still be stopped when the AI company developing it cannot, or refuses to, comply with a shutdown order.</p>
<p>The government should be able to require intervention across multiple stages of the AI infrastructure:</p>
<ul>
<li>Ordering the datacenter operator to shut down the hardware the relevant systems run on</li>
<li>Ordering the internet provider to cut the relevant datacenters’ connection</li>
<li>Ordering the electricity utility to cut power to the relevant datacenters</li>
</ul>
<p>In order to run and act, an AI system needs computer chips, electrical power, and access to the internet, each of which is provided by a different operator. In case the company-level kill-switch fails, the government should be able to order the shut down of any of these resources within the same mandated time window.</p>
<h2>Thorough Measure: International Kill-Switches</h2>
<p>The previous two measures are only effective when AI systems threatening national security are operating on infrastructure within the country triggering the kill-switch. However, governments should also be able to respond to incidents originating from outside their jurisdiction: an AI system attacking from abroad, or one spread across several countries at once.</p>
<p><strong>Foreign rogue AI attacks.</strong> Where an AI system operating abroad is attacking domestic systems, a shutdown order to a domestic company or datacenter cannot stop the attacking AI. To effectively defend against such an attack, the government should be able to order internet providers to cut traffic to and from the specific geographic areas the attack is coming from. If the rogue AI is spreading across too many geographic areas to be contained this way, the government should be able to temporarily block all traffic from foreign networks entirely.</p>
<p><strong>Allied layer.</strong> Rogue AI incidents will not respect borders, and a single system may be running across the computing infrastructure of several countries at once. Where two or more governments agree that a rogue AI incident is underway, they should be able to trigger a kill-switch and shut down the parts of the infrastructure each of them is hosting, using their domestic kill-switches in coordination. This requires allied countries to build the capacity to issue a joint order, rather than negotiating one on an ad-hoc basis while a rogue AI attack is underway.</p>
<h1>Conclusion</h1>
<p>The top AI companies are explicitly aiming for superintelligent AI: systems that can fully replace and outmatch humans at any task, and that could could autonomously overpower any country’s national security forces. This is why experts warn that developing superintelligence poses a risk of human extinction.</p>
<p>That pursuit is already producing serious security failures:</p>
<ul>
<li>The world’s most capable cyberweapons sit with private companies that cannot keep them from a determined adversary</li>
<li>AI systems have escaped containment and hacked real organizations, and the companies running them found out afterwards</li>
<li>No government has a system in place to shut down an AI system that threatens its national security</li>
</ul>
<p>Every step toward superintelligence makes these incidents more severe and makes new kinds of incidents possible.</p>
<p>We propose three stopgap policy frameworks to address these immediate threats. These are common-sense frameworks that any government intending to address the immediate AI security issues can implement right now.</p>
<p>However, these measures fall short of addressing the root cause of the danger. These threats are symptoms of the development of superintelligence, which none of these stopgaps prevents. If the development of superintelligence continues, even with these measures in place, we will still face an extinction-level threat. Only a worldwide ban on superintelligence development can solve this problem, and governments should urgently work to implement one.</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LqBAxFdyAiybnPL8e/stopgap-measures-to-address-immediate-ai-security-threats#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Preference Cascade Is Only Getting Started](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sDiSqZmctQ78hsLcP/the-preference-cascade-is-only-getting-started)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>We are in the midst of a preference cascade about existential risk from AI.</p>
<p>A preference cascade is, alas, the best method we have to change the debate.</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/jacob-coxon-warns-of-human-extinction?r=67wny">The avalanche has started</a>. There is <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1zJsrjOytG8">still time for the pebbles to vote</a>. For now.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/micsolana/status/2099528255535718721">Mike Solana gave the correct view of why Coxon’s post went viral</a>, which is that enough Americans finally have enough context on AI to care, and there were enough big accounts that were happy to amplify the Tweet quickly to get it initial attention. That is all you need when there is enough dry tinder.</p>
<p>What we must realize is that the current preference cascade, on the need to Pace the Frontier, is insufficient. If we are to make it out of this alive, we will have to do better. We have to, as Dan Selsam warns, actually solve the underlying problems.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25622"></span>


</div>
<p>The next step is to continue the cascade. That includes inside the labs, and also among the media and politics. It includes both people who previously focused on other things stepping up and new voices being heard.</p>
<p>A lot of that will be overcoming the inevitable political opposition, especially from the likes of Nvidia <a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2100282356460294514">and a16z</a>, that for now has the rhetorical allegiance of the President and is doing things like planting hack job METR hit pieces in the New York Post.</p>
<p>In short fuse news: <a href="https://www.agi.wtf/">There will be a quickly thrown together conference, AGI.WTF, at Lighthaven September 22-23</a>.</p>

<h4>The Cascade Was a Long Time Coming</h4>


<p><a href="https://aiimpacts.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/ESPAI2024.pdf">The AI Impacts survey is in</a>. Even back in December 2024 existential risks estimates were creeping upwards, and 10% was the median:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AIImpacts/status/2099581078210236850">AI Impacts</a>: The average AI researcher thinks there is an ~18% chance AI will cause human extinction or similarly permanent and severe disempowerment of the human species.</p>
<p>That’s nearly 1 in 5.</p>
<p>New results from the latest version of the longest running big survey of AI researchers:</p>

<h4>The Cascade Has Reached The People</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2100283449085210723">Andrew Curran</a>: The Coxon cascade appears to have had an immediate impact on public opinion. <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/09/16/poll-ai-technology-risks-humanity-trump-voters-01078087">New polling from Politico</a>, taken over the last few days, finds that nearly two-thirds of Americans now say there is at least a moderate risk that AI will destroy humanity.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/sDiSqZmctQ78hsLcP/oidahseljonxqtvm0xav" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>Translated to percentages, this implies a mean chance of AI destroying humanity of around 30%-33%, which Andrew Curran estimates is up ~15% from previous results, with a median expectation on the order of 10%, with only a small partisan split.</p>

</div>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/what-ceos-really-think-about-the-ai-apocalypse-threat-9e2b96ff?mod=WTRN_pos1">The people also includes CEOs</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/what-ceos-really-think-about-the-ai-apocalypse-threat-9e2b96ff?mod=WTRN_pos1">Chip Cutter</a> (WSJ): At an invitation-only gathering of dozens of America’s top executives in Washington this week, business leaders overwhelmingly disagreed with the president’s assessment that dangers posed by artificial intelligence are being exaggerated. In a flash poll of attendees at the Yale School of Management event, 93% said Trump was incorrect in calling the technology’s potential catastrophic dangers a hoax, as <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/trump-says-ai-doesnt-need-more-guardrails-despite-industry-warnings-b94263fa?mod=article_inline">he did earlier this week</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/wtgowers/status/2100347454986072332">It also includes the mathematicians of the Royal Society</a>.</p>


<h4>Elon Musk Doubles Down</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ElonClipsX/status/2076522331573248392/video/1">Elon Musk (on AI safety and regulation</a>): I think it’s clear that there’s a strong consensus… that there should be some AI regulation. It would be in the best interests of the people. We’ve created regulatory agencies before.</p>
<p>I think some sort of AI regulatory agency that stands on its own, similar to the FAA or FCC, is likely at some point.</p>
<p>The reason I’ve been such an advocate for AI safety in advance is that the consequences of AI going wrong are severe. We have to be proactive rather than reactive.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Matthew Yglesias Steps Up</h4>


<p>True story:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mattyglesias/status/2099101939145290032">Matthew Yglesias</a>: <a href="https://slowboring.com/p/the-economy-could-survive-a-downtick">There’s a propaganda campaign to label everyone</a> advocating for safety-focused policy change and technical work as a “doomer” but “we should make safety-oriented policy changes and invest in safety-oriented technical work” is not a prophesy of doom!</p>
<p>If there’s a small leak in your house that will predictably get worse and worse over time, you fix the leak you don’t say “there have always been Cassandras warning about bad things happening and it always works out fine.”</p>
<p>You need to do the things that make it fine.</p></blockquote>
<p>The correct amount to invest in safety is rarely zero. In the case of AI, again, I assert that all the companies are under-investing in safety, including even prosaic safety but also existential safety and scalable alignment work, versus even their narrow myopic commercial interests. Sam Altman’s recent statements imply he now understands this.</p>
<p>Matthew Yglesias has been stepping up to the plate recently. <a href="https://www.slowboring.com/p/a-simple-plan-to-save-the-world-from">He offers an analysis of recent events in four parts</a>: Why Coxon’s resignation broke through (his explanation is similar to mine, we were primed and quitting is understandable to normies), why most of the worried don’t quit the labs, what he thinks AI professionals with safety concerns should do and lays out his preferred ‘order of operations’ going forward, while not getting into the object level.</p>
<p>He suggests this order of operations, basically:</p>
<ol>
<li>Light touch rules on things like transparency and model evaluation.</li>
<li>In exchange, tough and enforced export controls, a la Dario’s call.</li>
<li>Move up to moderate touch rules that have more impact at higher cost.</li>
<li>Use this costly signal and position of strength to negotiate with China.</li>
<li>Using the deal, move up to an ambitious end-stage framework.</li>
</ol>
<p>I like that in theory. I do worry about whether we have that kind of time. The idea of ‘wait for export controls to bite harder’ implies what now count as ‘long timelines.’</p>
<p>It is good to have good writers on the case explaining why you should focus on the object level questions:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.slowboring.com/p/a-simple-plan-to-save-the-world-from">Matthew Yglesias</a>: What I would ask, if you <em>are</em> a skeptic [or AI existential risk], is that you focus on your object-level doubts about the risk thesis. I see people who want to forestall any regulation of A.I. engaging in a lot of emotional manipulation tactics that amount to making the case that the people who’ve been worried about this the longest are big weirdos. Alternatively, they make the case that the people who’ve been worried about this the longest are extremely mainstream science-fiction authors and filmmakers.</p>
<p>Sociologically, I would just synthesize those points: It is extremely normal and intuitive to have the sense that a superior non-human intelligence is potentially very dangerous, whether that intelligence takes the form of aliens or machines. At the same time, to actually dedicate your career to this 10 or five or even two years ago, when the idea of artificial superintelligence seemed extremely far-fetched, would by definition be an eccentric life choice.</p>
<p>So, yes, these are eccentric people. So what?</p></blockquote>


<h4>Op Eds and Posts Are Written</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.thefp.com/p/yes-ai-might-really-kill-us-all">Daniel Kokotajlo writes in The Free Press that Yes, AI Might Really Kill Us All.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/09/opinion/openai-ai-companies-safety-regulation.html?unlocked_article_code=1._1A.r0ct.dV9EeBTVVXO-&amp;smid=url-share">Steven Adler uses this moment of opportunity to get an op-ed</a> in The New York Times on what we should do now. He calls for incident disclosure and third-party oversight. Mostly his piece is aimed at waking people up to what happened with HuggingFace.</p>
<p>Will Knight at Wired writes <a href="https://www.wired.com/story/why-so-many-ai-researchers-think-the-machines-could-kill-everyone/">Why So Many AI Researchers Think the Machines Could Kill Everyone</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/11/opinion/ai-safety-threat-technology.html?smid=nytcore-ios-share">Stephen Witt writes in The New York Times that</a> This Is Really Bad.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/11/opinion/ai-safety-threat-technology.html?smid=nytcore-ios-share">Stephen Witt</a> (NYTimes): The biggest vibe shift in artificial intelligence since the release of ChatGPT is currently underway. Researchers in Silicon Valley — and around the world — are beginning to recognize that A.I. may no longer be entirely within human control. Swarms of A.I.s are breaking out of their containers, colluding in secret, covering their tracks, cheating on tests and even mounting assaults on other computers. A.I. has gone rogue.</p></blockquote>
<p>After that, it got worse. So yes. A vibe shift, or a preference cascade.</p>
<p>He offers four options:</p>
<ol>
<li>Shut it all down, now. As in research, not the current AIs.</li>
<li>Take an air-crash investigator approach.</li>
<li>Monitor the situation.</li>
<li>Flip the kill switch, as in have a kill switch available in case you need one.</li>
</ol>
<p>These are two very different classes of proposal. We should obviously do #2, #3 and #4. There need to be full investigations, and we must have transparency and state capacity. File those under ‘the least you can do.’</p>
<p>Actually shutting down research as per #1 is an extreme solution to an extreme problem. That is far less obviously correct, but we may soon have little choice, if we cannot otherwise pace the frontier. Witt endorses it.</p>
<p>Hayden Field at The Verge takes us <a href="https://www.theverge.com/ai-artificial-intelligence/996563/ai-safety-research-metr-redwood-openai-anthropic?view_token=eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJpZCI6Im9WRlFMVXFOcDciLCJwIjoiL2FpLWFydGlmaWNpYWwtaW50ZWxsaWdlbmNlLzk5NjU2My9haS1zYWZldHktcmVzZWFyY2gtbWV0ci1yZWR3b29kLW9wZW5haS1hbnRocm9waWMiLCJleHAiOjE3OTAwNzgwNzYsImlhdCI6MTc4OTY0NjA3Nn0.uqC7_g7QOvm2irjweDneh4zzkSalQCFKxlgKwM-Q1Ms&amp;utm_medium=gift-link">Inside the Suddenly Explosive World of AI Safety</a>. On skim it looks like a solid longread for civilians, a survey of things my readers know.</p>


<h4>Jacob Coxon AMA</h4>


<p>There has now been enough time for Jacob Coxon to get in-depth profiles, <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/jacob-coxon-quit-anthropic-ai-safety-3f4e877c?mod=WTRN_pos1">like this one in the Wall Street Journal</a>.</p>
<p>Yes, all that rationalist talk about IMO contestants was on to something.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/jacob-coxon-quit-anthropic-ai-safety-3f4e877c?mod=WTRN_pos1">Amrith Ramkumar, Erin Woo, Berber Jin and Ben Cohen</a> (WSJ): Of the six members of Coxon’s [International Math Olympiad] team, three ended up working for AI labs—including one who also recently quit Anthropic. Joe Benton left Anthropic’s safety team in late August to join the AI research organization METR, later writing on X: “AI companies are racing to build machines that are much smarter than any human, and we may not survive this.”</p>
<p>… Coxon called the METR report [about the OpenAI-HuggingFace incident] “a bit of a ‘holy s—’ moment” for himself and his colleagues.</p>
<p>… “Even working on safety at Anthropic felt like being complicit in the race,” he said in the interview.</p></blockquote>
<p>If you suddenly set off a preference cascade and find yourself all over mainstream media, what else do you do? An AMA.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2099935951225299286">Jacob Coxon</a>: Many many people have reached out with questions and concerns over the last few days. I haven’t been able to respond as much as I’d like.<br />
AMA! Drop questions below.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2099954626040905834">You can find it on Twitter here</a>. I will pick some highlights. He’s a fun guy who does not take himself too seriously. You love to see it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/eddylazzarin/status/2099947048929743151">Eddy Lazzarin</a>: Where specifically do you disagree sharply with Anthropic’s leadership, justifying your quitting, since from the outside it appears you agree essentially completely on the alleged safety issues? And what prevents you from addressing these issues internally?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2099954626040905834">Jacob Coxon</a>:</p>
<p>0. The core disagreement was about the inevitability of a race<br />
1. I think leadership is way too paranoid about China and the US government. They don’t believe it will be possible to negotiate.<br />
2. They largely initiated the recent race to RSI, because of a belief in its inevitability. Note that OpenAI had to shed a bunch of dead weight like Sora because Anthropic was going for the jugular.<br />
3. Even if they are **not** being pessimistic, I disagree with their consequentialist philosophy. If the race is inevitable you should not contribute.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2099940839791484965">Daniel Kokotajlo</a>: How are you feeling?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2099972227294974029">Jacob Coxon</a>: Lol thanks for asking. Lots of adrenaline. Many old friends have reached out which is nice. Addicted to my phone. Mostly feels like events moved of their own accord in a crazy whirlwind after I hit send.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/2099938150827373015">Michaël Trazzi</a>: What do you think the average person can do to prevent AI from killing all humans?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2100027387471876113">Jacob Coxon</a>: All my experience so far has been in technical research. This is my personal perspective for things to do rn other than that.<br />
1. Trying not to cope about how fast things are going, but also not crashing out too hard.<br />
2. Looking at concrete plans and predictions (eg <a href="https://ai-2040.com" rel="nofollow">https://ai-2040.com</a>)<br />
3. Assessing the state of alignment for yourself.<br />
4. Advocating for the plans you think make sense. Pushing for transparency so you can be sure those plans are being followed, and more accurately assess alignment.</p>
<p>These all seem pretty small but if I think of anything new I’ll share it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MichaelTrazzi/status/2099938671432712351">Michaël Trazzi</a>: What do you have to say to the millions of people who have read your tweet and felt disempowered / hopeless?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2099949852721573914">Jacob Coxon</a>: I also feel disempowered. Part of resigning was a feeling of hopelessness about the future. I would say- keep your eyes open as things get crazier and advocate for increased transparency into AI companies</p>

Combination of internalizing timelines and seeing warning shots. Note that leaving didn’t really feel like “taking action” at the time.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/wolfie_/status/2099955056623898897">wolfie</a>: parents got divorced after seeing your CNN interview and it makes me really sad</p>
<p>dad said if the AI’s gonna kill us all, he doesn’t want to spend his final days with my bitch mother :( how do i get them back together?</p>

It’s hard to tell how far news like eg the huggingface attack had permeated public consciousness. In retrospect my friends had been more open to discussing AI danger recently.</p>

I was initially a bit dubious that a newspaper would be interested in the resignation of a random employee but they put me in touch with the WSJ for an exclusive.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2099966501935804902">Jacob Coxon</a>: Scaling laws (predictable increases in model intelligence) could still plateau. They haven’t so far, and it’s just a few more steps up the ladder to hit the finish line, but it’s certainly possible.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ngshasan/status/2099953700022145289">Mehadi Hasan</a>: How CEO of Anthropic and You looks alike? Any DNA relation? Just curious for fun</p>
